<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Revised 2025 Apr 4; Received 2025 Mar 5; Accepted 2025 Apr 29; Issue date 2025 Aug.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">

<p>This is an open access article under the terms of the <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/</a> License, which permits use and distribution in any medium, provided the original work is properly cited, the use is non‐commercial and no modifications or adaptations are made.</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC12319508  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40351102/" class="usa-link">40351102</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>ABSTRACT</h2>
<p>Cranberry proanthocyanidins (CPACs) exert potent antiinflammatory and antibacterial activities in humans and anticancer effects in preclinical models, including those targeting esophageal adenocarcinoma (EAC). This study applied proteomic profiling to investigate CPACs’ inhibitory effects on reflux‐induced EAC in a rat model. Tandem mass spectrometry was applied to protein isolated from water‐, CPAC‐, and reflux‐exposed esophagi with and without CPAC treatment. Differentially expressed proteins were identified, followed by enrichment analyses to assess CPACs’ capacity to ameliorate reflux‐induced changes in gene set hallmarks, pathways, and process networks. CPAC directly reversed 42.1% of reflux‐induced protein alterations. Gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) revealed CPAC mitigated 11 hallmarks enriched in reflux‐induced EAC (i.e., oxidative phosphorylation, myogenesis, adipogenesis, MYC targets, and P53). Top pathways over‐represented with reflux and directly reversed by CPAC included spliceosome, metabolic pathways, and IL‐17 signaling. Transcription_mRNA processing, translation_regulation/initiation, and inflammation (i.e., kallikrein‐kinin system, neutrophil activation) dominated process networks upregulated by reflux and downregulated by CPAC. Networks exclusively altered by reflux and not mitigated by CPAC included ribosomal‐linked translation, immune response_antigen presentation, and leptin signaling. Similarly, CPAC did not reverse the reflux‐linked downregulation of ubiquinone metabolism. Identifying reflux‐induced cancer processes and pathways that CPAC fails to mitigate may inform opportunities for combination prevention efforts moving forward.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> cancer hallmarks, cancer interception, cranberry proanthocyanidins, esophageal adenocarcinoma, proteomics</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="mnfr70102-abs-0001"><hr class="headless">
<p>In this study we utilized proteomic profiling to investigate mechanisms linked to the cancer‐inhibitory effect of cranberry proanthocyanidins (CPACs) on reflux‐induced esophageal adenocarcinoma in a rat model. The results indicate that CPAC inhibits cancer through targeting multiple hallmarks of cancer known to be dysregulated in progression to esophageal cancer in humans.
</p>
<section class="bt xbox font-sm" id="boxed-text1"><p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ec5c/12319508/dc5a079ae3ec/MNFR-69-e70102-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-1" height="163" width="180" alt="graphic file with name MNFR-69-e70102-g003.jpg"></p></section></section><hr class="headless">
<h3>Abbreviations</h3>
<dl class="def-list" id="mnfr70102-dl-0001">
<dt>BE</dt>
<dd><p>Barrett's esophagus</p></dd>
<dt>CPAC</dt>
<dd><p>cranberry proanthocyanidins</p></dd>
<dt>EAC</dt>
<dd><p>esophageal adenocarcinoma</p></dd>
<dt>EGDA</dt>
<dd><p>esophagogastroduodenal anastomosis</p></dd>
<dt>ESCC</dt>
<dd><p>esophageal squamous cell carcinoma</p></dd>
<dt>FDR</dt>
<dd><p>false discovery rate</p></dd>

<section id="mnfr70102-sec-0010"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Esophageal cancer is the sixth leading cause of cancer‐related mortality worldwide and is the seventh leading cause of cancer mortality among males in the United States (US) [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0001">1</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0002" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0002">2</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0003" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0003">3</a>]. The two main histological types of esophageal cancer are esophageal squamous cell carcinoma (ESCC) and esophageal adenocarcinoma (EAC), each with unique etiologic and clinical features [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0004" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0004">4</a>]. Globally, ESCC rates have been declining following reductions in tobacco use [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0005">5</a>]. In contrast, EAC rates are increasing in many countries and represent the dominant histologic subtype in Westernized countries, including the US [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0003" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0003">3</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0005">5</a>]. Chronic reflux of gastric and duodenal contents, known as gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD), is the strongest known risk factor contributing to Barrett's esophagus (BE) and progression to EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0006" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0006">6</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0007" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0007">7</a>]. BE is the only recognized premalignant precursor lesion to EAC and is commonly treated with acid‐suppressing proton pump inhibitors or H2 blockers, yet nearly 50% of patients report incomplete responses and widespread use of acid‐suppressing medications has not translated into meaningful declines in EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0008" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0008">8</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0009" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0009">9</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0010" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0010">10</a>]. Chemotherapy and radiation, followed by surgery, remain the standard of care for patients who develop EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0011" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0011">11</a>]. However, less than 25% of EAC patients achieve a complete pathological response, and the 5‐year survival rate following a diagnosis of EAC remains dismal at about 20% [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0012" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0012">12</a>]. New efficacious strategies are desperately needed for both treating EAC and intercepting BE progression to EAC. Identifying a nontoxic efficacious agent or combination of agents that targets the major risk factors and molecular drivers associated with EAC progression would fill a substantial unmet need.</p>
<p>To this end, our laboratory has been investigating the cancer‐inhibitory capacity of cranberry polyphenols, mainly the proanthocyanidin fraction [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0013" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0013">13</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0014" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0014">14</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0015" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0015">15</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0016" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0016">16</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0017" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0017">17</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0018" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0018">18</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0019" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0019">19</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. Cranberry, a fruit rich in polyphenols, is known to possess strong antiinflammatory and antibacterial activities in human trials in addition to anticancer effects in multiple preclinical models, including EAC xenografts and a rat esophagogastroduodenal anastomosis (EGDA) model of reflux‐induced EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0014" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0014">14</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0015" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0015">15</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0016" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0016">16</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0021" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0021">21</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0022" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0022">22</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0023" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0023">23</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0024" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0024">24</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0025" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0025">25</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0026" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0026">26</a>]. The EGDA model results in reflux of bile and acidified stomach contents into the esophagus, mimicking GERD in BE patients, and supporting progressive pathological and molecular changes promoting EAC over time [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. Esophageal injury develops when gastric refluxate repeatedly interfaces with the unprotected esophageal epithelium, inducing cytokine‐mediated inflammation, immune cell migration, barrier dysfunction, DNA damage, genetic instability, and cancer progression [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0027" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0027">27</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0028" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0028">28</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0029">29</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0030">30</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0031" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0031">31</a>]. However, targeting progression to EAC has been challenging. Although EAC presents with a high mutational burden [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0032" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0032">32</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0033" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0033">33</a>], mutations are spread across a large number of genes with relatively low frequencies, except for <em>TP53</em>, which has a mutational frequency over 70% [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0034" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0034">34</a>], but to date has not been successfully targeted. EAC also presents with high spatial and temporal heterogeneity, acting as an additional obstacle to targeting mutational drivers in the context of cancer prevention [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0035">35</a>]. Beyond reflux and documented genomic alterations, the published literature supports that oral, gut, and local esophageal microbiota are altered in GERD, BE, and EAC, with numerous proinflammatory microbes increasing with pathologic progression to BE and EAC, reshaping the gut microbiome–esophageal metabolome axis [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0036" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0036">36</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0037" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0037">37</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0038" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0038">38</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0039" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0039">39</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0040">40</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0041">41</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0042" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0042">42</a>]. CPAC delivered in drinking water following reflux‐inducing EGDA significantly inhibits the development of high‐grade dysplasia and EAC by targeting reflux‐induced dysbiosis and mitigating bile acid metabolism and transport [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0019" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0019">19</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. In the rat EAC model, CPAC reverses reflux‐induced bacterial, inflammatory, and immune‐implicated changes that are similarly dysregulated in humans progressing from BE to EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. Microbial and metabolic profiling, coupled with transcriptional level changes, pointed to reflux‐induced alterations in Toll‐like receptor (TLR)/NF‐κB/TP53 signaling in the rat model, which CPAC potently mitigates [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. The current research extends these positive findings by comprehensively investigating protein alterations in the context of reflux‐driven EAC and evaluating CPAC's capacity to modulate specific proteins, pathways, processes, and cancer hallmarks. This research will inform mechanisms by which CPAC inhibits EAC, further establish the translational relevance of the model, and inform strategic mechanistically targeted combination treatments for greater cancer inhibitory efficacy.</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0020"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Experimental Section</h2>
<section id="mnfr70102-sec-0030"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. CPAC Preparation and Characterization</h3>
<p>The “Early Black” cultivar of cranberry fruit (<em>Vaccinium macrocarpon</em> Ait.) was collected at the Marucci Center for Blueberry and Cranberry Research (Chatsworth, NJ), followed by purification and extraction using solid‐phase chromatography, as previously described [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0043" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0043">43</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0044" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0044">44</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0045" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0045">45</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0046" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0046">46</a>]. Briefly, cranberries were homogenized in 70% aqueous acetone and CPAC concentrated under reduced pressure, purified, and isolated using bioassay‐directed fractionation. Spectrophotometry, <sup>13</sup>C NMR, electrospray mass spectrometry, matrix‐assisted laser desorption/ionization time‐of‐flight mass spectrometry, and acid‐catalyzed degradation with phloroglucinol were used to measure the concentration of CPAC extract and confirm the purity of isolated compounds. Freeze‐dried CPAC was stored at −80°C.</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0040"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Rat Reflux‐Induced EAC Model</h3>
<p>The animal study was performed using male Sprague‐Dawley rats and approved by the Institutional Laboratory Animal Care and Use Committee at the Medical College of Wisconsin (AUA3095). EGDA was performed, resulting in reflux‐induced EAC in male Sprague‐Dawley rats, as previously reported [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. In short, rats were randomized and treated with either water or CPAC in the drinking water ad libitum starting 1 week after the EGDA surgery and continuing through study completion as recently described [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. The previous weeks’ actual consumption of CPAC in the drinking water (based on pair‐housed rats) was utilized to estimate the concentration of CPAC required to deliver the target amount of 700 µg/rat/day which resulted in actual delivery of 690 µg/rat/day [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. At 25 and 40 weeks, rats were sacrificed. At 25 weeks of study, a total of 23 rats or 5–8 per group were utilized primarily for imaging studies and early histopathology assessments. At 40 weeks of study, a total of 53 rats were utilized to characterize changes in histopathology, for proteomic profiling as described herein and other molecular analysis as previously reported [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. Specifically, 16, 10, 11, and 16 rats completed 40 weeks of study in the water, CPAC, Reflux, and Reflux + CPAC groups, respectively. Esophageal tissues were isolated, with part fixed in neutral buffered formalin routinely processed, embedded on edge in paraffin blocks, ≥25 sections prepared with every 5th slide H&amp;E stained for histopathology characterization [20]. The remainder of the tissue was flash‐frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80°C until downstream processing and analysis.</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Untargeted Proteomic Profiling of Rat Esophageal Tissue</h3>
<p>Protein extraction and peptide identification were performed by the University of Michigan Proteomics and Peptide Synthesis Core using snap‐frozen rat esophageal tissue. Three samples per group (Water, CPAC, Reflux, and CPAC + Reflux) were processed (20 µg/sample) and separated on a 10% Bis‐Tris NuPAGE mini‐gel and run approximately 5 cm. Each gel lane was excised into 20 equally sized bands and processed by in‐gel digestion. Half of each gel digest was analyzed by nano LC‐MS/MS with a Waters NanoAcquity HPLC system interfaced to a ThermoFisher Q Exactive mass spectrometer. The mass spectrometer was operated in data‐dependent mode, with the Orbitrap operating at 70 000 full width at half maximum (FWHM) and 17 500 FWHM for MS and MS/MS, respectively.</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0060"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Proteomics Data Processing and Analysis</h3>
<p>Data were searched using a local copy of Mascot, and Mascot DAT files were parsed into Scaffold for validation, filtering, and creating a nonredundant list per sample. Data were filtered using a 1% protein and peptide false discovery rate (FDR) and requiring at least two unique peptides per protein. Differentially expressed proteins were identified using two‐sample <em>t</em> tests. Heatmap was generated using the ComplexHeatmap package (version 2.13.1) in R (version 4.2.0) [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0047" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0047">47</a>]. The UpSet plot was generated using the iPathwayGuide (AdvaitaBio Corporation, <a href="https://ipathwayguide.advaitabio.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://ipathwayguide.advaitabio.com</a>).</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0070"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Protein Isolation and Western Blot Analysis</h3>
<p>Protein isolation was performed as previously reported [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. Protein levels in collected lysates were quantified using the DC Protein Assay (Bio‐Rad, Hercules, CA), and 15 µg of proteins were loaded into 4%–20% or 10% Criterion TGX Precast Gels (Bio‐Rad). Immunoblotting was performed using the following commercially available antibodies: S100A9 (1:1000, #PA1‐46489, Invitrogen, Waltham, MA), MCM2 (1:750, #4007, Cell Signaling Technology, Danvers, MA), eIF4A (1:750, #2425, CST), IGFBP6 (1:750, #PA5‐115397, Invitrogen), and GAPDH (1:25 000, #2118, CST). Images were acquired using the ChemiDoc MP Imaging System (Bio‐Rad), and quantification was performed using the Image Lab Software (version 6.1, Bio‐Rad). Protein expression was normalized to the loading control (GAPDH), with fold‐change from the Reflux group reported.</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0080"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Enrichment Analysis</h3>
<p>An overrepresentation test of the significantly differentially expressed genes/proteins was performed using the WEB‐based Gene SeT AnaLysis Toolkit [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0048" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0048">48</a>]. The Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) database was used for the overrepresentation test to determine KEGG pathways overrepresented [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0049" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0049">49</a>]. Local network cluster analysis was performed using STRING by uploading the list of genes/proteins identified in specific pathways from the overrepresentation test [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0050">50</a>]. Process network analysis and pathway map enrichment analysis were performed using MetaCore (Clarivate Analytics, Philadelphia, PA, <a href="https://clarivate.com/products/metacore" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://clarivate.com/products/metacore</a>), as previously published [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. Significantly altered proteins (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05 and FC ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67) were used to evaluate process networks and enriched pathway maps uniquely altered by CPAC and reflux, as well as process networks and enriched pathways altered by reflux and directly reversed by CPAC. Gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) was performed using the GSEA software (version 4.2.2, Broad Institute, Boston, MA), as previously described [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0051" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0051">51</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0052" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0052">52</a>]. In short, ranking scores were calculated and imported into the GSEA software. GSEA analysis was then performed using the Hallmark gene set in the Molecular Signatures Database (MSigDB) with default parameters (1000 permutations, min size: 15, and max size: 500) [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0051" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0051">51</a>]. Rat genes were mapped to human orthologs before analysis. Hallmark pathways with <em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 and FDR ≤ 0.25 were considered statistically significant. A Venn diagram of unique and shared Hallmark pathways was generated using InteractiVenn [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0053" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0053">53</a>]. Bar plots of top protein expression changes in different pathways were generated using GraphPad Prism (version 10.2.2, GraphPad Software, Boston, MA, <a href="http://www.graphpad.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">www.graphpad.com</a>).</p></section></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0090"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results and Discussion</h2>
<section id="mnfr70102-sec-0100"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. CPAC Mitigates Reflux‐Induced Histopathological Changes in the Esophagus</h3>
<p>As summarized in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0001" class="usa-link">1</a>, CPAC delivered in the drinking water [690 µg/rat/day] significantly increases the percentage of histologically normal esophagus at both 25 and 40 weeks of study [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. CPAC simultaneously significantly inhibits high‐grade dysplasia and EAC by about 54% and 82%, respectively, at 40 weeks. CPAC acts to slow cancer progression, arresting pathological progression at the stage of hyperplasia and low‐grade dysplasia. Our research team recently published on CPAC's inhibitory effects on esophageal carcinogenesis with effects attributed to CPAC's prebiotic activity and modulation at the microbiome–metabolome axis [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>]. Herein, we extend our initial research findings to include CPAC's effect on the proteome in the context of reflux‐induced EAC and investigate reflux‐induced proteins that CPAC fails to mitigate to identify potential agents that can be logically combined with CPAC to enhance EAC inhibition. Beyond PPIs, pharmacologic agents postulated to hold chemopreventive activity targeting BE progression to EAC include nonsteroidal antiinflammatory drugs and statins, but to date, results have been negative or inconclusive, and concerns remain regarding negative side effects given long‐term use [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0054" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0054">54</a>].</p>

</div></section></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0110"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Proteins Significantly Altered in Reflux‐Induced EAC and Mitigated by CPAC</h3>
<p>Untargeted proteomic profiling was conducted on the four experimental groups consisting of Water, CPAC, Reflux, and CPAC + Reflux with Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1A</a> showing the dendrogram based on the hierarchical clustering of the four treatment groups. The dendrogram supports that the esophageal samples from the Reflux‐treated group sort closely together, as do the CPAC + Reflux samples, with the Water‐ and CPAC‐derived samples intermingled. The hierarchical clustering and <em>Z</em>‐scores further illustrate variations in protein expression based on treatment group, with the Reflux group clearly sorting from the other three groups, particularly contrasting with the CPAC + Reflux group. The Venn diagram in Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1B</a> and UpSet plot in Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1C</a> show the number of significantly upregulated and downregulated proteins within each group and shared dysregulation among the different treatment groups (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05 and FC ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67). The greatest number of differentially expressed proteins (<em>n</em> = 492) were detected between the Reflux and Water group, with 322 upregulated and 170 downregulated. Comparing the CPAC + Reflux versus Reflux group, 379 proteins were differentially expressed, with 104 upregulated and 275 downregulated. The fewest number of differentially expressed proteins were detected comparing the CPAC and Water group, totaling 128. Still, CPAC altered cancer‐linked proteins in normal‐appearing esophagi, supporting modulation of select proteins before risk‐escalating exposure to reflux. In the CPAC group, a number of proteins with cancer inhibitory functions including TAXBP3 (alias TIP‐1), SCP2, and SRP68 were upregulated [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0055">55</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0056" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0056">56</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0057" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0057">57</a>]; whereas, EXOSC2, MPO, LAMA1, SMURF1, or proteins linked to tumor promotion through alterations in cell cycle, migration, and polarity were significantly downregulated [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0058" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0058">58</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0059" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0059">59</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0060" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0060">60</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0061" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0061">61</a>]. Highly relevant to cancer interception are the 207 proteins, or 42.1% of proteins, dysregulated by Reflux and directly reversed by CPAC. In addition, the 285 proteins, altered by reflux induction but not reversed by CPAC, provide clues into pathways, processes, and agents that may be combined with CPAC for greater cancer inhibitory effects.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="mnfr70102-fig-0001"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ec5c/12319508/b97a7d287a66/MNFR-69-e70102-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-3" height="350" width="546" alt="FIGURE 1"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/mnfr70102-fig-0001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Untargeted proteomic profiling reveals proteins significantly altered in reflux‐induced EAC and mitigated by CPAC. (A) Hierarchical clustering reveals strong clustering based on Reflux, CPAC, or CPAC + Reflux treatment (<em>n</em> = 3/treatment). (B) Venn diagram showing the number of significantly altered proteins (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05 and fold‐change ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67) unique to each treatment group and proteins shared among groups. (C) UpSet plot showing the number of significantly upregulated and downregulated proteins within each group and shared dysregulation among the different treatment groups. Upregulated proteins are indicated by the “+” sign, whereas downregulated proteins are indicated by the “−” sign. Blue lines indicate intersections among groups. CPAC, cranberry proanthocyanidins.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0120"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Over‐Represented KEGG Pathways Altered by Reflux and Directly Mitigated by CPAC</h3>
<p>Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a> shows the KEGG pathways identified as dysregulated by Reflux and directly mitigated by CPAC. Based on the 207 identified proteins (Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1B</a>), Reflux upregulated 162 proteins and downregulated 45, all significantly mitigated with directionally reversed with CPAC treatment. The top KEGG pathways altered by Reflux‐induced EAC and mitigated by CPAC (<em>p</em> and FDR ≤ 0.05) included the spliceosome, terpenoid backbone biosynthesis, and metabolic pathways. Additional cancer‐linked pathways dysregulated with Reflux and restored with CPAC included IL‐17 signaling, proteoglycans in cancer, cell cycle, VEGF signaling, adherens junction, and chemical carcinogenesis (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05). The specific probes identified in our data set are shown in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a>. Additionally, Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2A–D</a> depict changes in several specific proteins in the spliceosome, metabolic, IL‐17, and cell cycle pathways, each implicated in human cancer and, in turn, of interest to target for inhibiting progression to EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0062" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0062">62</a>]. Aberrant splicing is increasingly recognized as a hallmark of cancer, with alternative splicing exhibited in most tumors, including EAC and BE [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0063" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0063">63</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0064" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0064">64</a>]. We previously reported significant isoform switching events, one form of dysregulated splicing, with pathological progression from BE with low‐grade dysplasia to BE high‐grade dysplasia, supporting a role for spliceosome changes in cancer progression [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0065" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0065">65</a>]. Mechanisms by which splicing perturbations promote cancer include increased cell proliferation, decreased apoptosis, altered autophagy machinery, enhanced cell migration, evasion of immune surveillance, and chemotherapeutic resistance [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0066" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0066">66</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0067" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0067">67</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0068" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0068">68</a>]. Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a> and Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2A</a> show specific spliceosome markers altered in reflux‐induced EAC and mitigated by CPAC with multiple DDX, hnRNP, and SRFS family members altered, as similarly reported in human BE and EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0069">69</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0070">70</a>]. DDX family members are not only sensors and regulators of RNA transcription, but have documented roles in antiviral responses, inflammation induction, altered innate immunity, and potentially TLR signaling [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0071" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0071">71</a>]. Interestingly, upregulated in human and rat EAC is DHX15, an RNA helicase involved in mRNA processing, antiviral, and antibacterial innate immunity [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0069">69</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0070">70</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0072" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0072">72</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0073" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0073">73</a>]. Similarly, DDX5, one of the specific family members altered in humans and the rat EAC model and directly mitigated by CPAC, is a transcriptional activator for TP53, the most mutated tumor suppressor in EAC and linked to BE progression to EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0034" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0034">34</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0069">69</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0074">74</a>]. The latter findings are consistent with CPAC mitigating the induction of multiple inflammatory signaling molecules and TLR family members in the rat EAC model in conjunction with reshaping the gut microbiome and local esophageal metabolome as reported [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>], but without assessing alterations in spliceosome signaling. Although our study focused on CPAC mitigating reflux‐induced spliceosome changes in the context of cancer prevention, emerging human evidence supports that select spliceosome factors in the hnRNP and SRSF families hold prognostic value [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0070">70</a>]. Specifically, high levels of hnRNPM and SRSF1 in EAC compared to BE tissues are significantly linked to reduced patient survival [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0070">70</a>]. Additionally, upregulation of spliceosomal and ribosomal proteins resulted in a spliceosome signature associated with cancer aggressiveness and poor prognosis among ESCC patients [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0075" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0075">75</a>].</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/mnfr70102-tbl-0002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="mnfr70102-tbl2-note-0001"><p>
<em>Note</em>: The 207 probes significantly elevated by Reflux and directly mitigated by CPAC based on a <em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 and fold‐change ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67 were included in the overrepresentation analysis determining enriched KEGG pathways. In data/total reflects the number of probes in the data set/the total number of probes in the pathway.</p></div></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="mnfr70102-fig-0002"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ec5c/12319508/8fa0c5af63a1/MNFR-69-e70102-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-5" height="982" width="612" alt="FIGURE 2"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/mnfr70102-fig-0002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Overrepresented KEGG pathways altered by Reflux and directly mitigated by CPAC. Individual proteins induced by Reflux and reversed by CPAC in (A) spliceosome pathway, (B) metabolic pathways, (C) IL‐17 pathway, and (D) cell cycle pathway. Local network cluster enrichment of (E) spliceosome pathway and (F) metabolic pathways. *<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05 and **<em>p</em> ≤ 0.01, two‐sample <em>t</em> test. CPAC, cranberry proanthocyanidins; KEGG, Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Next, STRING analysis was conducted on the 13 spliceosome and 38 metabolism probes altered in reflux and directly reversed by CPAC (Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a>) to gain additional insight regarding the specific types of spliceosome and metabolic changes, as shown in Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2A, E</a> and Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2B, F</a>, respectively. The top spliceosome changes and those with the highest magnitude of shift included mRNA splicing—major pathway and hnRNPM, nuclear localization signal; followed by mixed RS domain binding, and negative regulation of mRNA splicing via the spliceosome; and spliceosomal complex, and RS domain binding. Targeting the spliceosome to intercept or treat cancer with small molecule inhibitors has faced challenges due to off‐target effects given the spliceosomes’ critical role in many normal cellular functions and cancer. Thus, a natural nontoxic agent like CPAC that can target cancer‐induced spliceosome changes with eliciting toxicity to normal cells offers advantages and could serve as the basis for further development of targeted nontoxic small molecule inhibitors, particularly as spliceosome structural elucidation advances. As shown in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a>, the largest number of proteins altered (<em>n</em> = 38) were linked to the metabolic pathway with select proteins induced by reflux and mitigated by CPAC, depicted in Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2B</a>. STRING analysis was conducted, revealing that the top metabolic changes focused on one‐carbon metabolic processes targeting amino acids of the serine family, folate, sulfur amino acid metabolism and choline, as well as cholesterol biosynthesis, cytochrome p450 and steroid biosynthetic process, fatty acid beta oxidation and heme biosynthesis, among others. Altered metabolism has also emerged as a cancer hallmark, with one‐carbon metabolism being essential for providing the building blocks for DNA, amino acids, and other molecules required to meet the energy requirements of cancer cells [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0076" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0076">76</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0077" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0077">77</a>]. These cellular changes align with those detected in BE and EAC, showing that the major metabolic shift favors increased reliance on glycolysis for energy production, accompanied by alterations in lipid metabolism, mitochondrial dysfunction, and activation of cancer‐permissive signaling pathways [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0078" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0078">78</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0079" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0079">79</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0080" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0080">80</a>]. Exposure‐driven risk factors such as bile and gastric acid, as well as an obese phenotype, act as contributors supporting inflammation and transformation of normal squamous esophageal cells into more intestinal‐like cells with a propensity for malignancy. As an example, G6PD is tightly linked to energy metabolism as the first and rate‐limiting enzyme of the pentose phosphate pathway (PPP) [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0081">81</a>]. G6PD is induced by acid exposure in vitro and is elevated in EAC tumors compared to normal esophageal mucosal tissues [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0082" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0082">82</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0083" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0083">83</a>]. These results are consistent with other studies reporting enhanced G6PD expression and PPP flux in various cancers, and correlation with cancer progression through multiple cancer hallmarks, including reprogramming cellular metabolism, sustaining proliferative signaling, resisting cell death, and activating invasion and metastasis [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0084" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0084">84</a>]. In turn, targeting G6PD is attractive for cancer inhibition, particularly if nontoxic efficacious agents can be identified that permit administration over long periods of time without side effects.</p>
<p>The IL‐17 signaling pathway was another top pathway significantly induced in reflux‐induced EAC and mitigated by CPAC, as detailed in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a> with specific proteins shown in Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2C</a> (i.e., DEFB4, MAPK3, MAPK14, S100A8, S100A9, and SRSF1). IL‐17 is a cytokine released by multiple cell types of adaptive and innate immunity and is elevated in BE and implicated in BE to EAC progression [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0085" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0085">85</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0086" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0086">86</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0087" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0087">87</a>]. S100A8 and S100A9, with roles in spliceosome signaling, are elevated at the protein and RNA level in EACs compared to normal squamous epithelium [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0088" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0088">88</a>]. Limited research supports that IL‐17 plays a role in promoting the progression of EAC by contributing to inflammation within the tumor microenvironment (TME) and that IL‐17A may contribute to poor patient prognosis [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0089" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0089">89</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0090" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0090">90</a>]. In vitro studies reveal that IL‐17 signaling impacts tumor cell migration, invasion, and therapeutic resistance [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0091" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0091">91</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0092" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0092">92</a>].</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0130"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. Pathway Maps Altered in Reflux‐Induced EAC With and Without CPAC Mitigation</h3>
<p>Pathway maps (via Metacore) were assessed to identify those pathways up‐ or downregulated by reflux‐induced EAC and directly reversed by CPAC and to assess those pathways altered in reflux‐induced EAC and that CPAC fails to significantly mitigate. Based on the 207 proteins directly mitigated by CPAC, 51 pathway maps were identified as upregulated (<em>p</em> and FDR ≤ 0.05), with the top 20 depicted in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0003" class="usa-link">3</a> and the full list in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S1</a>. Top pathways included translation‐regulation of translation initiation, followed by cytoskeleton remodeling‐keratin filaments and DNA damage‐ATM/ATR regulation of G1/S checkpoint. Other key cancer‐linked pathways upregulated by reflux and directly downregulated by CPAC included a number of pathways involved in immune responses and proinflammatory signaling pathways (i.e., NF‐κB, MAPK, and EGFR) as well as metabolism and adhesion, all pathways implicated in EAC in humans [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0080" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0080">80</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0093" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0093">93</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0094" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0094">94</a>]. There were no significantly downregulated pathway maps in Reflux that were directly reversed by CPAC based on both a <em>p</em> value and FDR cutoff at 0.05, likely in part due to the smaller number of downregulated probes and the dominate number of upregulated proteins.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="mnfr70102-tbl3-note-0001"><p>
<em>Note</em>: Pathway enrichment analysis of 162 probes upregulated in Reflux and directly downregulated by CPAC (<em>n</em> = 162/207, <em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 and fold‐change ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67) identify 51 enriched pathway maps with the top 20 displayed (<em>p</em> value and FDR ≤ 0.05). Table <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S1</a> shows the full list of 51 pathway maps upregulated. In data/total reflects the number of probes in the data set/the total number of probes in the pathway map.</p></div></div></section><p>Next, the 285 proteins altered by reflux and not directly reversed by CPAC were analyzed, resulting in six pathway maps upregulated and seven downregulated as detailed in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0004" class="usa-link">4</a>. The top pathway maps upregulated by Reflux and not directly reversed by CPAC included immune response‐antigen presentation by MHC Class 1, classical pathway, which could potentially be targeted to restore endogenous MHC‐I responses in an effort to inhibit cancer progression. However, exploitation of the MHC‐1 pathway for cancer inhibition is in its early stages, with several ongoing clinical trials that will inform future targeting efforts [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0095" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0095">95</a>]. Putative pathways of activation of the classical complement system in major depressive order were the second top pathway upregulated by reflux and not reversed by CPAC. Although similarly immune‐linked, it is comprised of different proteins (i.e., C4, C4A, and C4B). Targeting C4‐demethylating genes in the cholesterol pathway sensitizes cancer cells to EGF receptor inhibitors [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0096" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0096">96</a>], but whether such an agent synergizes with CPAC remains unknown. The top downregulated pathway maps induced by Reflux and not directly reversed by CPAC include transport‐the role of AVP in regulation of Aquaporin 2 and renal water absorption with specific proteins including protein kinase members, again offering potential new agents to test with CPAC for enhanced cancer inhibitory capacity. Additional pathway maps upregulated by Reflux without mitigation by CPAC with cancer relevance included cell adhesion, ubiquinone metabolism, and a shift from oxidative to glycolytic muscle fiber phenotype. Knowledge of pathways and related proteins may be applied for improved combination treatments targeting EAC inhibition. These results also inform the overlap between the rat reflux‐induced EAC model and alterations reported in human EAC progression and, in turn, highlightling the preclinical models translational relevance.</p>

</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/mnfr70102-tbl-0004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="mnfr70102-tbl4-note-0001"><p>
<em>Note</em>: Pathway enrichment analysis of 285 probes exclusively altered in Reflux (<em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 and fold‐change ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67) reveals six pathways upregulated and seven pathways downregulated in Reflux and not directly reversed by CPAC (<em>p</em> value and FDR ≤ 0.05). In data/total reflects the number of probes in the data set/the total number of probes in the pathway map.</p></div></div></section></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0140"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Process Networks Altered in Reflux‐Induced EAC With and Without CPAC Reversal</h3>
<p>Process network analysis was additionally performed in Metacore to identify processes altered by Reflux‐induced EAC and directly reversed by CPAC. As shown in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0005" class="usa-link">5</a>, seven process networks were significantly (<em>p</em> and FDR ≤ 0.05) upregulated by Reflux and reversed by CPAC, which include transcription (mRNA processing), translation (initiation and regulation of initiation), inflammation (Kallikrein‐kinin system, histamine signaling, and neutrophil activation), and cell cycle (S phase). Table <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S2</a> provides a complete list of the 39 process networks upregulated by Reflux and directly reversed CPAC (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05). Table <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S3</a> shows the full list of 13 process networks downregulated in Reflux and directly mitigated by CPAC. Dysregulation of transcription, translation, and cell cycle is reported in numerous types of cancer, including EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0097" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0097">97</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0098" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0098">98</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0099" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0099">99</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0100">100</a>]. Spliceosome, identified in Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a>, is part of the mRNA processing machinery [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0101" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0101">101</a>]. Significant upregulation of mRNA processing and reversal by CPAC further support that CPAC can mitigate dysregulated spliceosome machinery in EAC. Protein translation is another critical step often upregulated in cancer, which can be divided into three main steps: initiation, extension, and termination. Translation initiation is considered the rate‐limiting step of translation. During translational initiation, formation of the translation initiation complex (eIF4F), consisting of eIF4A, eIF4E, and eIF4G, is the most important step [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0097" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0097">97</a>]. Multiple proteins in translation initiation factor complexes, including eIF4A, eIF2S1, and eIF3S5, were significantly upregulated in Reflux and downregulated by CPAC treatment, supporting that CPAC directly mitigated the expression of key translation initiation factor complexes and, therefore, affected protein translations in reflux‐induced EAC. Chronic inflammation is an important component for EAC development [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0102">102</a>]. Numerous proinflammatory chemokines and transcription factors, such as IL‐6, NF‐κB, and TGF‐β, are found to be elevated in EAC compared to squamous epithelium [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0103">103</a>]. The presence of proinflammatory chemokines further creates an immunosuppressive TME by recruiting immune cells, such as neutrophils, to TME [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0104">104</a>]. The cancer‐promoting role of neutrophils has been well‐documented in cancer [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0105">105</a>]. Neutrophils play a multifaceted role in cancer progression, metastasis, and recurrence [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0105">105</a>]. Neutrophils are recruited and activated by IL‐8 [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0106">106</a>], and in EAC, increased levels of IL‐8 have been observed in BE with high‐grade dysplasia and EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0107" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0107">107</a>]. In addition, the IL‐8‐neutrophil immune microenvironment was pinpointed as an ethnicity‐influenced driver of BE to EAC transformation [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0108" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0108">108</a>]. The significant upregulation of neutrophil activation is in alignment with published literature, and CPAC was able to mitigate neutrophil activation through the modulation of the IL‐8‐MAPK signaling axis [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0109">109</a>]. Moreover, dysregulation of the cell cycle has previously been reported to be associated with BE progression to EAC, with proteins such as MCM2 and PCNA being dysregulated in EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0020">20</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0110">110</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0111">111</a>]. Significant upregulation of both MCM2 and PCNA was observed in Reflux‐induced EAC and CPAC significantly reversed the expression of both proteins.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/mnfr70102-tbl-0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="mnfr70102-tbl5-note-0001"><p>
<em>Note</em>: The 207 probes significantly altered by Reflux and directly mitigated by CPAC (<em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 and fold‐change ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67) were included in this process network enrichment analysis showing networks with a <em>p</em> value and FDR ≤ 0.05. A full list of networks based on a <em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 is included in Tables <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S2</a> and <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S3</a>. In data/total reflects the number of probes in the data set/the total number of probes in the process network.</p></div></div></section><p>Three process networks were significantly downregulated in Reflux and upregulated in CPAC, which include muscle contraction, skeletal muscle development, and neuromuscular junction. Limited research suggests that dysregulation of skeletal muscle‐related processes may be related to cachexia, a complication commonly observed in cancer patients [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0112">112</a>], but merits further research in the context of cancer prevention.</p>
<p>Process Networks significantly altered by Reflux, which CPAC failed to mitigate, were also identified (Table <a href="#mnfr70102-tbl-0006" class="usa-link">6</a>), revealing some common networks to those directly reversed by CPAC. Surprisingly, several identified Common Processed Networks upregulated included muscle contraction, skeletal muscle development, and neuromuscular junction; whereas, common networks downregulated included translation initiation and mRNA processing. However, genes/proteins identified in each common Process Network differed. For example, CPAC significantly reversed the upregulation of several proteins in translation initiation factor complexes in Reflux‐induced EAC, whereas it fails to mitigate upregulated ribosomal proteins also involved in translation initiation, such as RPS8, RPL7, and PRL35. Moreover, Process Network analysis identified several unique pathways not significantly mitigated by CPAC treatment, including leptin signaling, integrin‐mediated cell‐matrix adhesion, translation elongation, protein folding, and antigen presentation. The data suggests these pathways could potentially be targeted in combination with CPAC to further increase the efficacy of CPAC. Future studies will focus on identifying FDA‐approved agents that could target pathways that CPAC fails to mitigate and investigate whether a combination treatment of CPAC and identified agent has synergistic effects. Tables <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S4</a> and <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S5</a> fully report those process networks up‐ and downregulated by Reflux and not directly reversed by CPAC with a <em>p</em> value cutoff at 0.05.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="mnfr70102-tbl6-note-0001"><p>
<em>Note</em>: The 285 probes significantly altered by Reflux and not directly mitigated by CPAC (<em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 and fold‐change ≥ 1.5 or ≤ 0.67) were included in process network enrichment analysis showing networks with a <em>p</em> value and FDR ≤ 0.05. A full list of networks based on a <em>p</em> value ≤ 0.05 is included in Tables <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S4</a> and <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S5</a>. In data/total reflects the number of probes in the data set/the total number of probes in the process network.</p></div></div></section></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0150"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Hallmark Gene Sets Altered Based on Treatment Groups</h3>
<p>Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3A</a> summarizes identified hallmark gene sets (based on MSigDB) altered in the treatment comparisons and shows select GSEA plots linked to altered hallmarks. Overall, Reflux induction resulted in significant alterations in 16 hallmarks (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05 and FDR ≤ 0.25), with 11, or nearly 69%, of the hallmarks directly mitigated by CPAC in the context of Reflux. All of the hallmarks induced by Reflux in the rat EAC model have been reported to similarly be significantly altered in human EAC, with the exception of allograft rejection [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0113" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0113">113</a>]. Key Reflux‐stimulated hallmarks downregulated in Reflux‐induced EAC and directly upregulated by CPAC included oxidative phosphorylation, myogenesis, and adipogenesis. Tables <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S5–S7</a> provide additional details for each hallmark comparison, showing <em>p</em> and FDR values and normalized enrichment scores indicative of directionality. Figures <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S1</a> and <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S2</a> show individual proteins implicated in the specific hallmarks. Reflux‐induced EAC resulted in the enrichment of multiple hallmarks implicated in the reprogramming of energy metabolism, a phenomenon documented in progression to cancer, cancer migration, and patient prognosis through impacting the response to therapy [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0113" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0113">113</a>]. The human literature supports that oxidative phosphorylation is downregulated in EAC [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0078" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0078">78</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0079" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0079">79</a>]. Specific proteins implicated in oxidative phosphorylation significantly downregulated with by Reflux and upregulated by CPAC included NDUFC2, NDUFS1, ALDH6A1, and SDHB. All four of these markers have been linked to gastrointestinal cancers [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0114" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0114">114</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0115">115</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0116">116</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0117" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0117">117</a>]. The SDHB gene is involved in two important energy conversion pathways, the citric acid cycle and oxidative phosphorylation, and has tumor suppressive functions [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0118" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0118">118</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0119" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0119">119</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0120" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0120">120</a>]. Mutations in SDHB increase tumorigenesis, metastasis, and infer poor prognosis for select cancers [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0121" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0121">121</a>], but have not been reported in human esophageal cancer. EAC does share a number of molecular alterations with gastric cancers [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0122" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0122">122</a>], where NDUFC2, NDUFS1, and ALDH6A1 are reportedly dysregulated [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0114" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0114">114</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0115">115</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0116">116</a>]. Low expression of <em>Aldh6a1</em> predicts patient prognosis and response to immune therapy among patients with gastric cancer [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0116">116</a>]. NDUFS1 is the largest subunit of the mitochondrial Complex I and has a key role in maintaining its stability and function. NDUFS1 catalyzes the first step of nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NADH) oxidation of the respiratory chain within the mitochondria. In gastric carcinomas, a low level of NDUFS1 is correlated with large tumor size, poor differentiation, presence of lymph node metastasis, high TNM stage and is associated with the poor prognosis of GC patients [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0115">115</a>]. NDUFC2, which encodes the C2 subunit of Complex I enzyme in oxidative phosphorylation, is less well characterized, but like NDUFS1, it was induced by reflux and downregulated by CPAC. Our data is consistent with the limited body of data supporting that cranberry constituents alleviate oxidative‐stress dependent mitochondrial dysfunction [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0123" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0123">123</a>]. Two other hallmarks downregulated in the Reflux‐induced EAC model include myogenesis and adipogenesis, both reportedly downregulated in esophagogastric junctional tumors in humans [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0069">69</a>]. CPAC significantly reversed these hallmarks by mitigating contributory proteins as shown in Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3B</a> and Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S1</a>. Hallmarks upregulated by Reflux and directly downregulated by CPAC included MYC targets V1, MYC targets V2, G2M checkpoint, E2F Targets, unfolded protein response, MTORC1 signaling, P53 pathway, and allograft rejection (in order of significance). MYC, the top upregulated hallmark in the data, is dysregulated in more than 50% of cancers, including human EAC and BE precursor lesions [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0069">69</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0124" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0124">124</a>, <a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0125" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0125">125</a>]. Regulation of MYC is considered a potentially important cancer target, but pharmaceutical inhibition of this protein has yet to be achieved. MYC has many diverse functions from controlling transcription and alternative mRNA splicing to regulating gene transcription, as well as playing a role in DNA synthesis and protein translation and metabolism [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0124" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0124">124</a>]. Thus, targeting downstream MYC effector pathways may offer a more viable alternative. As shown in Figure <a href="#mnfr70102-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3A</a> and Table <a href="#mnfr70102-supinfo-0001" class="usa-link">S6</a>, multiple additional hallmark gene sets implicated in human BE and EAC progression (G2M, E2F targets, unfolded protein response, MTORC1, and P53 signaling) were upregulated in the rat Reflux‐induced model and downregulated with CPAC treatment. Hallmarks uniquely detected in Reflux‐induced EAC without direct mitigation by CPAC included downregulated hypoxia and KRAS signaling DN, upregulated cholesterol homeostasis, protein secretion, and IL2/STAT5 signaling. In humans, significantly altered hallmarks reported in EAC compared to normal esophageal mucosa include cholesterol homeostasis, protein secretion, and IL2/STAT5 [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0069">69</a>]. In the rat Reflux‐induced EAC model, hypoxia was also reduced, suggesting that reduced oxidative phosphorylation may be linked to alterations in the electron transport chain itself [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0126" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0126">126</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="mnfr70102-fig-0003"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ec5c/12319508/a62bcfe49bba/MNFR-69-e70102-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-7" height="579" width="546" alt="FIGURE 3"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/mnfr70102-fig-0003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Hallmark gene sets enriched by Reflux or CPAC treatment alone or in combination. (A) Hallmarks and representative GSEA plots induced by Reflux and directly reversed by CPAC (<em>n</em> = 11) and hallmark pathways and representative enrichment plots exclusively altered by Reflux (<em>n</em> = 5) and CPAC (<em>n</em> = 3). Hallmark pathways are ranked by <em>p</em> value and FDR with <em>p</em> &lt; 0.05 and FDR &lt; 0.25 considered significant. (B) Individual proteins induced by Reflux and reversed by CPAC in the hallmarks of MYC targets V1, Adipogenesis, and MTORC1 signaling. *<em>p</em> &lt; 0.05 and **<em>p</em> &lt; 0.01, two‐sample <em>t</em> test. (C) Western blots of select proteins altered by Reflux and mitigated by CPAC. CPAC, cranberry proanthocyanidins; FDR, false discovery rate; GSEA, gene set enrichment analysis.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Hallmarks exclusively upregulated by CPAC in the context of Reflux have also been reported in human EAC cases, including bile acid metabolism, fatty acid metabolism, and xenobiotic metabolism [<a href="#mnfr70102-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mnfr70102-bib-0069">69</a>]. In brief, many hallmarks are altered in common in human EAC and the rat Reflux‐induced EAC model, supporting the model's suitability for understanding cellular processes, pathways, and associated mechanisms contributing to EAC progression as well as screening agents with cancer inhibitory potential in the context of GERD.</p></section></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0160"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Concluding Remarks</h2>
<p>The prognosis for those diagnosed with EAC remains dismal for several reasons, including the failure of widespread screening and late‐stage diagnosis. Additionally, we lack predictive models and molecular or biochemical signatures that can be broadly applied for improved risk stratification among patients with BE. Finally, efficacious agent‐based interventions that can be delivered to BE patients over long periods of time in an effort to intercept BE progression to EAC remain elusive. As discussed above, numerous hallmark gene sets have been identified that are dysregulated during progression to EAC with many pharmacologic agents actively being developed to target the oncogenic drivers implicated in EAC. Still, efforts have been thwarted by off‐target effects and drug toxicity. The current research supports that CPAC, a nontoxic derivative of cranberries, may offer a viable and efficacious alternative. One that is amenable to regular long‐term consumption as part of the regular diet yet imparts protection against progression to HGD and EAC through its activity toward multiple proteins, signaling pathways, and hallmarks implicated in the pathogenesis to EAC. Finally, identifying the cancer‐linked processes and pathways effectively targeted by CPAC provides a foundation for informing mechanistically driven agent combinations for intercepting progression to EAC.</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0180"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Conflicts of Interest</h2>
<p>The authors declare no conflicts of interest.</p></section><section id="mnfr70102-sec-0210"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Peer Review</h2>

<li id="mnfr70102-bib-0010">
<span class="label">10.</span><cite>
